of Weinberg’s factorization properties derived for
\Lambda=0
.

Looking towards flat holography

The holographic principle experienced its most brilliant realization through the AdS/CFT correspondence. Even at the classical level, we observe that all of the ingredients are present to ensure its good functioning, namely the conformal compactness of the bulk spacetime bounded by a codimension one spacelike surface on which a clear notion of chronology can be defined everywhere. Otherwise, in the flat case, an analogous holographic correspondence is much less known and mastered, mainly because of the null nature of the conformal boundary. Even if the conformal compactification process can be performed, there is no more SFG expansion allowing to parametrize the gravitational phase space uniquely by codimension one boundary data in a transparent fashion. This thesis reviewed the usual method to approach null infinity, which consists in following the directions of null cones in the bulk that precisely define the Bondi gauge null foliation. In (A)dS, we provided all technicities to translate the boundary data commonly used in holography to Bondi radiative fields, at least in four dimensions. We also proposed a well-defined flat limit process within the Bondi gauge at the level of the phase space, which is enough to obtain all dynamical information about the bulk gravity that we need to build a more robust notion of flat holography. We have shown that the renormalization of the asymptotically flat spacetime can be understood as a relic of the holographic renormalization procedure in (A)dS coupled to a careful management of the corner terms before pushing the cosmological constant to zero. We believe that all of the technical elements to build a consistent notion flat holography on the shoulders of the famous AdS/CFT correspondence have been derived through to the
\Lambda
-BMS program.

We plan to pursue this research direction in the future. Crucial progress toward this endeavor has been made recently, namely the study of the coadjoint representation of BMS
4

[
347
]
under which the non-radiative solution space in the Bondi gauge transforms. In three-dimensional gravity genuinely exempt of radiation, the coadjoint representation of BMS
3
is necessary and sufficient to encapsulate the whole solution space
[
39
]
, and the theory has been shown to be perfectly described by a geometric action
[
348
]
for a theory living on the boundary. The inclusion of radiation in four-dimensional gravity would amount to couple the dual geometric action defined on the coadjoint orbits of BMS
4
spanning the stationary subsector of the theory to external sources encoded in the asymptotic shear and Bondi news tensors. This would lead to a holographic description of asymptotically flat gravity at null infinity.

Gauge theories with external sources

Beyond its concrete contributions to a better understanding of the asymptotic symmetries of General Relativity, this thesis was an opportunity to gather knowledge about the leaky phase spaces that can be constructed for this theory and to emphasize their importance in the theoretical discussion of the phenomena involving gravitational radiation. Although this is a very old research topic first initiated by Bondi, Metzner, Sachs and van der Burg almost 60 years ago, several technical results are still missing or incompletely understood in the literature. For instance, gauge theories with non-conserved and non-integrable charges need more than ever renewed attention from a purely mathematical point of view. The algebraic properties of the surface charges as postulated by Barnich and Troessaert for the very restricted case of the BMS
4
algebra
[
30
]
were re-analyzed very recently considering radiative gravity as a Hamiltonian open system (see
e.g.

[
266
,
90
,
88
]
) with more care given to the treatment of sources of non-conservation and non-integrability. New prescriptions for the charge bracket in the presence of anomalies due to background structures have also been studied
[
91
,
92
]
. In the future, we would like to extend the analysis to any gauge theory coupled with external sources, with a framework that clearly separates the dynamical degrees of freedom that contribute to the physical flux and are responsible for non-integrability as well as non-conservation of the charges among all gauged degrees of freedom. These investigations could help in proving the conjecture of
[
259
]
, discussed in section
2.2.3.4
for gravity. Moreover, considering the low number of hypotheses and the generality of the developments presented in section
2.3.2
, we would also like to address the relevance of the Barnich-Troessaert bracket for theorizing the dynamics of open systems in classical Hamiltonian and Lagrangian mechanics, where dissipative phenomena and exchanges with the environment in the grand-canonical ensemble are often incorporated by hand in the equations of motion without completely controlling the modified phase space features and symmetries disturbed by the presence of the external sources. This ambitious research endeavor has already been started
[
94
]
, encouraged by the current stimulating interest around these fundamental problematics.

A few last words

This concludes our exploration of leaky covariant phase spaces in General Relativity as well as four years of intense personal and professional enrichment. The exercise of completing a Ph.D. thesis is not a series of solitary computations, but a living adventure during which one acquires a more solid technical mastery while sharing ideas and projects with wonderful people. I hope that all doctoral students will have as fruitful and pleasant an experience as I had. Although what lies ahead contains that bundle of unexpectedness and hope that makes it so appealing, it should never be forgotten that it is built on the beautiful experiences and fond memories of the past. In writing these last lines I now take the step to forever put my studies behind me, with a firm desire to do justice to what I have been taught and to continue to contribute honorably to a quest definitely promised a bright future!
\blacksquare

Appendices

C   Killing vectors of (A)dS
4
in Bondi gauge

In this appendix, we review the expression of the Killing vectors for the global vacua of 4
d
gravity (Minkowksi, (A)dS
4
) in the Bondi gauge. Once the results have been presented for AdS
4
(with radius
\ell
), one can readily extrapolate to the dS
4
case thanks to the analytical continuation
\ell\to i\ell
. The isometries of flat space are finally obtained by taking the flat limit
\ell\to+\infty
.

For the purpose to compute the symmetries of AdS
4
, it is convenient to see this spacetime isometrically immersed into
\mathbb{R}^{(2,3)}
as the hypersurface

\{X^{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{(2,3)}|-X_{0}^{2}-X_{\underline{0}}^{2}+X_{1}^{2}+X_{2}^{2}+X_{3}^{2}=-\ell^{2}\},

(C.1)

see section
3.1.1
for a review. In this point of view, the exact symmetry group of AdS
4
is the homogeneous part of the isometry group of
\mathbb{R}^{(2,3)}
,
ISO(2,3)
, which is
SO(2,3)
. The generators of
SO(2,3)
algebra

\mathcal{J}_{ab}=\mathcal{J}_{[ab]}=X_{b}\partial_{a}-X_{a}\partial_{b},

(C.2)

a,b\in\{0,\underline{0},1,2,3\}
, directly lead to the Killing vectors of AdS
4
after pullback on (
C.1
).

In retarded Bondi coordinates
(u,r,x^{A})
, the AdS
4
line element takes the form (
4.2.46
) where we take
x^{A}=(z,\bar{z})
as the stereographic coordinates on the 2-sphere with unit-round metric
\mathring{q}_{AB}
. The Minkowski metric in retarded coordinates (
5.1.1
) is recovered in the flat limit
\ell\rightarrow\infty
. In these coordinates, the Killing vectors are given by
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
with

\mathcal{J}_{ab}^{u}=f,\qquad\mathcal{J}_{ab}^{r}=-\frac{r}{2}\mathring{D}_{A}\mathcal{J}_{ab}^{A},\qquad\mathcal{J}_{ab}^{A}=Y^{A}-\frac{1}{r}\partial^{A}f

(C.3)

where
\mathring{D}_{A}
is the covariant derivative on the sphere and the functions
f,Y^{A}
obey
[
222
]

\partial_{u}f=\frac{1}{2}\mathring{D}_{A}Y^{A}\,,\qquad\partial_{u}Y^{A}=\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}\partial^{A}f\,,\qquad 2\mathring{D}_{(A}Y_{B)}-\mathring{q}_{AB}\mathring{D}_{C}Y^{C}=0.

(C.4)

The last equation fragments the set of Killing vectors of AdS
4
in two categories, namely the 4 vectors associated with exact isometries of the boundary cylinder (with
\mathring{D}_{A}Y^{A}=0
):

1.

Rotations:

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{12}&=[0,0,-iz,-i\bar{z}]=\partial_{\phi},\\
\mathcal{J}_{13}&=\Big[0,0,\frac{1}{2}(-1-z^{2}),\frac{1}{2}(-1-\bar{z}^{2})\Big],\\
\mathcal{J}_{23}&=\Big[0,0,\frac{1}{2}i(-1+z^{2}),\frac{1}{2}i(-1+\bar{z}^{2})\Big];\end{split}

(C.5)

2.

Time translation:

\mathcal{J}_{0\underline{0}}=\partial_{u};

(C.6)

and the 6 vectors associated with the conformal isometries of the boundary cylinder, for which
\mathring{D}_{A}Y^{A}\neq 0
:

3.

Boosts on the first timelike coordinate: with the shorthand notation
\mu(u/\ell,r)

\equiv

r

\cos(u/\ell)

+

\ell\sin(u/\ell)
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{01}&=\Big[\frac{(z+\bar{z})\ell\sin(\frac{u}{\ell})}{1+z\bar{z}},-\frac{(z+\bar{z})\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{(1+z\bar{z})},\frac{(-1+z^{2})\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{2r},-\frac{(-1+\bar{z}^{2})\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{2r}\Big],\\
\mathcal{J}_{02}&=\Big[-\frac{(z-\bar{z})\ell\sin(\frac{u}{\ell})}{1+z\bar{z}},\frac{i(z-\bar{z})\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{(1+z\bar{z})},-\frac{i(1+z^{2})\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{2r},\frac{i(1+\bar{z}^{2})\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{2r}\Big],\\
\mathcal{J}_{03}&=\Big[\frac{(-1+z\bar{z})\ell\sin(\frac{u}{\ell})}{1+z\bar{z}},\frac{(-1+z\bar{z})\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{(1+z\bar{z})},-\frac{z\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{r},-\frac{\bar{z}\mu(\frac{u}{\ell},r)}{r}\Big];\\
\end{split}

(C.7)

4.

Boosts on the second timelike coordinate: by virtue of the
SO(2,3)
algebra,

\mathcal{J}_{\underline{0}i}=-[\mathcal{J}_{0\underline{0}},\mathcal{J}_{0i}]=-\partial_{u}\mathcal{J}_{0i}.

(C.8)

The Lorentz algebra can be explicitly checked for the rotations
V_{i}={\epsilon_{i}}^{jk}\mathcal{J}_{jk}
together with the boosts along
0
time direction,
K_{i}=\mathcal{J}_{0i}
, or the boosts along
\underline{0}
time direction,
K_{i}=\ell\mathcal{J}_{\underline{0}i}
:

[V_{i},V_{j}]=\epsilon_{ijk}V_{k}\,,\quad[K_{i},K_{j}]=-\epsilon_{ijk}V_{k}\,,\quad[V_{i},K_{j}]=\epsilon_{ijk}K_{k}.

(C.9)

The time translation and the three rotations have a trivial flat limit. When
\ell\to+\infty
the boosts
\mathcal{J}_{0i}
become the Lorentz boosts

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{01}&\rightarrow\Big[\frac{u(z+\bar{z})}{1+z\bar{z}},-\frac{(r+u)(z+\bar{z})}{1+z\bar{z}},\frac{(r+u)(-1+z^{2})}{2r},\frac{(r+u)(-1+\bar{z}^{2})}{2r}\Big],\\
\mathcal{J}_{02}&\rightarrow\Big[-i\frac{u(z-\bar{z})}{1+z\bar{z}},i\frac{(r+u)(z-\bar{z})}{1+z\bar{z}},-i\frac{(r+u)(1+z^{2})}{2r},i\frac{(r+u)(1+\bar{z}^{2})}{2r}\Big],\\
\mathcal{J}_{03}&\rightarrow\Big[\frac{u(-1+z\bar{z})}{1+z\bar{z}},-\frac{(r+u)(-1+z\bar{z})}{1+z\bar{z}},-\frac{(r+u)z}{r},-\frac{(r+u)\bar{z}}{r}\Big],\end{split}

(C.10)

while the boosts
\mathcal{J}_{\underline{0}i}
become the spatial translations

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{\underline{0}1}&\rightarrow\Big[\frac{z+\bar{z}}{1+z\bar{z}},-\frac{z+\bar{z}}{1+z\bar{z}},\frac{-1+z^{2}}{2r},\frac{-1+\bar{z}^{2}}{2r}\Big],\\
\mathcal{J}_{\underline{0}2}&\rightarrow\Big[-i\frac{z-\bar{z}}{1+z\bar{z}},i\frac{z-\bar{z}}{1+z\bar{z}},-i\frac{1+z^{2}}{2r},i\frac{1+\bar{z}^{2}}{2r}\Big],\\
\mathcal{J}_{\underline{0}3}&\rightarrow\Big[-\frac{1-z\bar{z}}{1+z\bar{z}},\frac{1-z\bar{z}}{1+z\bar{z}},-\frac{z}{r},-\frac{\bar{z}}{r}\Big].\end{split}

(C.11)

One can check explicitly that they verify the constraints (
C.4
) in the limit
\ell\to+\infty
.

D   Map from Bondi to Starobinsky/Fefferman-Graham gauge

This appendix contains the detailed material complementing the section
4.2.2
. It is devoted to discuss the change of coordinates that maps a general vacuum asymptotically locally (A)dS
4
spacetime (
\Lambda\neq 0
) in the Bondi gauge to the Starobinsky/Fefferman-Graham gauge. In particular, we give here the explicit forms of the various coefficients appearing in the perturbative change of coordinate (
4.2.49
) in terms of the metric fields.

We use the following shorthand notations for subleading fields in the Bondi gauge:

\begin{split}\frac{V}{r}&=\frac{\Lambda}{3}e^{2\beta_{0}}r^{2}+r\ V_{(1)}(t,x^{A})+V_{(0)}(t,x^{A})+\frac{2M}{r}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}),\\
U^{A}&=U_{0}^{A}(t,x^{B})+\frac{1}{r}U_{(1)}^{A}(t,x^{B})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}U_{(2)}^{A}(t,x^{B})+\frac{1}{r^{3}}U_{(3)}^{A}(t,x^{B})+\mathcal{O}(r^{-4}),\\
\beta&=\beta_{0}(t,x^{A})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\beta_{(2)}(t,x^{A})+\mathcal{O}(r^{-4}).\end{split}

(D.1)

whose explicit on-shell values can be read off in (
4.2.23
) and (
4.2.20
). That allows to write the equations in a more compact way. All fields are now evaluated on
(t,x^{A})
since the boundary time coordinate can be defined as
t
as well as
u
. We also define some recurrent structures appearing in the diffeomorphism as differential operators on boundary scalar fields
\varphi(t,x^{A})
,
\chi(t,x^{A})
:

\begin{split}P[\varphi]&=\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\beta_{0}}(\partial_{t}\varphi+U_{0}^{A}\partial_{A}\varphi),\\
Q[\varphi;\chi]&=P[\varphi]-2P[\chi]\varphi,\\
B_{A}[\varphi]&=\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\beta_{0}}(\partial_{A}-2\partial_{A}\beta_{0})\varphi.\end{split}

(D.2)

Below,
P^{n}[\varphi]
denotes
n
applications of
P
on
\varphi
, for example
P^{2}[\varphi]\equiv P[P[\varphi]]
. Now we can write down the perturbative change of coordinate to Starobinsky/Fefferman-Graham gauge:

\displaystyle R_{1}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{\Lambda},

\displaystyle R_{2}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{2\Lambda^{2}}e^{-2\beta_{0}}V_{(1)},

\displaystyle R_{3}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2\Lambda}\beta_{(2)}-\frac{3}{\Lambda^{2}}\Big(1+\frac{3}{4}e^{-2\beta_{0}}V_{(0)}\Big)+\frac{27}{2\Lambda^{3}}\Big(Q[V_{(1)};\beta_{0}]-\frac{3}{8}e^{-4\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle R_{4}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{\Lambda^{2}}e^{-2\beta_{0}}\Big(M+2e^{4\beta_{0}}P[\beta_{(2)}]-\frac{5}{2}V_{(1)}\beta_{(2)}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0pt\quad-\frac{9}{\Lambda^{3}}\Big\{Q[V_{(0)};\beta_{0}]+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\beta_{0}}\Big[U^{A}_{(1)}\partial_{A}V_{(1)}-2V_{(1)}U^{A}_{(1)}\partial_{A}\beta_{0}-3V_{(1)}(2e^{2\beta_{0}}+V_{(0)})\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0pt\quad+\frac{27}{\Lambda^{4}}e^{2\beta_{0}}\Big[P^{2}[V_{(1)}]-2V_{(1)}\Big(P^{2}[\beta_{0}]+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\beta_{0}}Q[V_{(1)};\beta_{0}]-\frac{3}{32}e^{-8\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}^{2}\Big)-2P[\beta_{0}]P[V_{(1)}]\Big],

\displaystyle T_{1}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-2\beta_{0}})R_{1}(t,x^{A}),

\displaystyle T_{2}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-2\beta_{0}})R_{2}(t,x^{A})-\frac{18}{\Lambda^{2}}\Big(P[\beta_{0}]-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}\Big),

\displaystyle T_{3}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-2\beta_{0}})R_{3}(t,x^{A})-\frac{3}{\Lambda^{2}}e^{-2\beta_{0}}(1+e^{-2\beta_{0}}V_{(0)}-2\partial^{A}\beta_{0}\partial_{A}\beta_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{9}{\Lambda^{3}}e^{-2\beta_{0}}\Big(Q[V_{(1)};\beta_{0}]-4e^{4\beta_{0}}P^{2}[\beta_{0}]-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle T_{4}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-2\beta_{0}})R_{4}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{9}{2\Lambda^{2}}\Big[e^{-4\beta_{0}}\Big(M-\beta_{(2)}V_{(1)}-\frac{1}{3}U_{(2)}^{A}\partial_{A}\beta_{0}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}(P[\beta_{(2)}]-8\beta_{(2)}P[\beta_{0}])\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{27}{\Lambda^{3}}\Big\{\frac{1}{8}e^{-2\beta_{0}}\Big(3Q[V_{(0)};\beta_{0}]-\frac{8}{3}P[\beta_{0}]V_{(0)}-2e^{-4\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}V_{(0)}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{3}e^{-2\beta_{0}}\Big(P[U_{(1)}^{A}]\partial_{A}\beta_{0}+\frac{3}{2}U_{(1)}^{A}\partial_{A}P[\beta_{0}]\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{12}e^{-4\beta_{0}}\Big[U_{(1)}^{A}B_{A}[V_{(1)}]+6V_{(1)}-2(V_{(1)}\partial_{A}\beta_{0}+2\partial_{B}\beta_{0}\partial_{A}U_{0}^{B})\partial^{A}\beta_{0}\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{81}{\Lambda^{4}}\Big\{-\frac{2}{3}e^{4\beta_{0}}\Big(P^{3}[\beta_{0}]+2P[\beta_{0}]P^{2}[\beta_{0}]\Big)+\frac{1}{4}\Big(P^{2}[V_{(1)}]-2V_{(1)}P^{2}[\beta_{0}]\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{6}P[\beta_{0}]\Big[\Big(\frac{13}{4}e^{-4\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}-8P[\beta_{0}]\Big)V_{(1)}+P[V_{(1)}]\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{16}e^{-4\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}\Big(5P[V_{(1)}]-e^{-4\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}^{2}\Big)\Big\},

\displaystyle X_{1}^{A}(t,x^{B})

\displaystyle=(T_{1}-R_{1})U_{0}^{A},

\displaystyle X_{2}^{A}(t,x^{B})

\displaystyle=(T_{2}-R_{2})U_{0}^{A}-\frac{3}{2\Lambda}e^{-2\beta_{0}}U_{(1)}^{A}+\frac{9}{\Lambda^{2}}P[U_{0}^{A}],

\displaystyle X_{3}^{A}(t,x^{B})

\displaystyle=(T_{3}-R_{3})U_{0}^{A}+\frac{1}{\Lambda}e^{-2\beta_{0}}U_{(2)}^{A}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{6}{\Lambda^{2}}\Big[Q[U_{(1)}^{A};\beta_{0}]+\frac{1}{2}B^{A}[V_{(1)}]+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\beta_{0}}(U_{(1)}^{B}\partial_{B}U^{A}_{0}-V_{(1)}U_{(1)}^{A})\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{18}{\Lambda^{3}}e^{2\beta_{0}}Q[P[U_{0}^{A}];\beta_{0}],

\displaystyle X_{4}^{A}(t,x^{B})

\displaystyle=(T_{4}-R_{4})U_{0}^{A}-\frac{3}{4\Lambda}e^{-2\beta_{0}}\Big[U_{(3)}^{A}+\frac{1}{2}e^{2\beta_{0}}(\partial^{A}\beta_{(2)}-8\beta_{(2)}\partial^{A}\beta_{0})\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{9}{2\Lambda^{2}}\Big[Q[U_{(2)}^{A};\beta_{0}]-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\beta_{0}}\Big(V_{(1)}U_{(2)}^{A}-\frac{1}{3}U_{(2)}^{B}\partial_{B}U_{(0)}^{A}\Big)-2\beta_{(2)}P[U_{0}^{A}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{4}B^{A}[V_{(0)}]+\frac{1}{2}C^{AC}B_{C}[V_{(1)}]+\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\beta_{0}}U_{(1)}^{C}B_{C}[U^{A}_{(1)}]\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{27}{\Lambda^{3}}\Big\{e^{2\beta_{0}}\Big(P[Q[U_{(1)}^{A};\beta_{0}]]+P[B^{A}[V_{(1)}]]-\frac{1}{2}q^{AC}P[B_{C}[V_{(1)}]]\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\beta_{0}}\Big[V_{(1)}P[U_{(1)}^{A}]-\frac{2}{3}\Big(P[U_{(1)}^{C}]+\frac{1}{2}B^{C}[V_{(1)}]-5P[\beta_{0}]U_{(1)}^{C}\Big)\partial_{C}U_{0}^{A}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}P[V_{(1)}]U_{(1)}^{A}-\frac{2}{3}(V_{(0)}-8e^{2\beta_{0}}\partial^{B}\beta_{0}\partial_{B}\beta_{0})P[U_{0}^{A}]-U_{(1)}^{B}P[\partial_{B}U_{0}^{A}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}(\partial^{A}U_{0}^{C})B_{C}[V_{(1)}]\Big]+3P[\beta_{0}]V_{(1)}\partial^{A}\beta_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-e^{-4\beta_{0}}\Big[\frac{3}{32}\Big(\partial^{A}(V_{(1)}^{2})-\frac{20}{3}\partial^{A}\beta_{0}V_{(1)}^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{6}(V_{(1)}\partial^{B}\beta_{0}-\partial^{C}\beta_{0}\partial_{C}U_{0}^{B})\partial_{B}U_{0}^{A}\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle\hskip-3.0pt+\frac{81}{\Lambda^{4}}\Big\{\frac{1}{3}e^{4\beta_{0}}P^{3}[U_{0}^{A}]+\Big[\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\beta_{0}}V_{(1)}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q[V_{(1)};\beta_{0}]-\frac{4}{3}e^{4\beta_{0}}(P^{2}[\beta_{0}]+P[\beta_{0}]^{2})\Big]P[U_{0}^{A}]\Big\}.

The function
R_{1}
is not constrained by the gauge conditions (
4.2.48
) but is simply fixed by requiring that the Weyl rescalings of the transverse metric on the sphere agree between both gauges,
i.e.

g^{(0)}_{AB}=q_{AB}
. Several consistency checks can be performed at each stage of the computation. The boundary metric in the SFG gauge must be equivalent to the pulled-back metric on the hypersurface
\{r\to\infty\}
in the Bondi gauge, up to the usual replacement
u\to t
. This is the case because of (
4.2.50
). At subleading orders,
g^{(1)}_{ab}
and
g^{(2)}_{ab}
must be algebraically determined by
g_{ab}^{(0)}
and its first and second derivatives, what turns out to be the case. The constraint (
4.2.24
) enforces
g^{(1)}_{ab}=0
while the annulation of
\mathcal{D}_{AB}(t,x^{C})
(
4.2.25
) results in

g^{(2)}_{ab}=\frac{3}{\Lambda}\Big[R^{(0)}_{ab}-\frac{1}{4}R_{(0)}g^{(0)}_{ab}\Big].

(D.3)

This agrees with the
d=3
version of the equation of motion (
3.1.33
). We will not give the full general form of
g^{(3)}_{ab}
, but it can be proven that this tensor is traceless with respect to
g^{(0)}_{ab}
and that the equations of motion in the Bondi gauge are necessary and sufficient to show its conservation
D^{(0)}_{a}g_{(3)}^{ab}=0
, as we argued in the main text.

After partial boundary gauge fixing
\beta_{0}=0
,
U_{0}^{A}=0
(Gaussian normal coordinates on the boundary), but leaving
\sqrt{q}
arbitrary, the expressions of each coefficient in the diffeomorphism simplifies drastically:

\displaystyle R_{1}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{\Lambda},

\displaystyle R_{2}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{2\Lambda^{2}}V_{(1)},

\displaystyle R_{3}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2\Lambda}\beta_{(2)}-\frac{3}{\Lambda^{2}}\Big(1+\frac{3}{4}V_{(0)}\Big)+\frac{27}{2\Lambda^{3}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}V_{(1)}-\frac{3}{8}V_{(1)}^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle R_{4}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{\Lambda^{2}}\Big(M+\partial_{t}\beta_{(2)}-\frac{5}{2}V_{(1)}\beta_{(2)}\Big)-\frac{9}{\Lambda^{3}}\Big[\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}V_{(0)}-\frac{3}{4}V_{(1)}(2+V_{(0)})\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{27}{\Lambda^{4}}\Big(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{t}^{2}[V_{(1)}]-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{t}V_{(1)}^{2}+\frac{6}{32}V_{(1)}^{3}\Big).

\displaystyle T_{1}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle T_{2}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{2\Lambda^{2}}V_{(1)},

\displaystyle T_{3}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{\Lambda^{2}}(1+V_{(0)})+\frac{9}{2\Lambda^{3}}\Big(\partial_{t}V_{(1)}-V_{(1)}^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle T_{4}(t,x^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{2\Lambda^{2}}\Big[M-\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{t}\beta_{(2)}+4V_{(1)}\beta_{(2)})\Big]+\frac{9}{\Lambda^{3}}\Big[-\frac{9}{16}\partial_{t}V_{(0)}+\frac{3}{2}V_{(1)}(1+\frac{1}{2}V_{(0)})\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{81}{\Lambda^{4}}\Big(\frac{1}{16}\partial_{t}^{2}V_{(1)}-\frac{5}{64}\partial_{t}V_{(1)}^{2}+\frac{1}{16}V_{(1)}^{3}\Big).

\displaystyle X_{1}^{A}(t,x^{B})

\displaystyle=X_{2}^{A}(t,x^{B})=0,

\displaystyle X_{3}^{A}(t,x^{B})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Lambda}U_{(2)}^{A}-\frac{3}{2\Lambda^{2}}\partial^{A}V_{(1)},

\displaystyle X_{4}^{A}(t,x^{B})

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{4\Lambda}\Big(U_{(3)}^{A}+\frac{1}{2}\partial^{A}\beta_{(2)}\Big)+\frac{9}{2\Lambda^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}U_{(2)}^{A}-\frac{1}{2}V_{(1)}U_{(2)}^{A}+\frac{1}{8}\partial^{A}V_{(0)}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{27}{16\Lambda^{3}}\ q^{AB}\Big(\partial_{t}\partial_{B}V_{(1)}+\frac{1}{2}V_{(1)}\partial_{B}V_{(1)}\Big).

In particular,
t=u+\mathcal{O}(\rho)
and both time coordinates can be identified on the boundary.

Note.

The expressions of the diffeomorphism and the metric elements
g_{ab}^{(0)}
,
g_{ab}^{(2)}
and
g_{ab}^{(3)}
(the latter within the additional boundary gauge fixing) can be found in the
Mathematica
file
BMS_TO_FG.nb
attached to
[
271
]
on the
arXiv
publication. The package
RGtensors

[
349
]
is needed to run this notebook.

E   Useful relations for computations in the Bondi gauge

In this appendix, we give some useful relations in the Bondi gauge that are widely used in sections
2.2.4
,
2.3.3
and
4.3.3
. The metric on the celestial sphere is written as
q_{AB}
. From
\delta q^{AB}=-q^{AC}q^{BD}\delta q_{CD}
, we have

T_{AB}\delta q^{AB}=-T^{AB}\delta q_{AB}.

(E.1)

for any symmetric tensor
T_{AB}
. For any
C_{AB}
symmetric traceless tensor (
i.e.

C_{AB}=C_{(AB)}
,
q^{AB}C_{AB}=0
), it follows that

q_{AB}\delta C^{AB}=C_{AB}\delta q^{AB}.

(E.2)

Requiring further that
\delta\sqrt{q}=0
one finds

\displaystyle\delta(C_{AB}C^{AB})

\displaystyle=2C_{AB}\delta C^{AB},

(E.3)

\displaystyle\delta(C_{AB}C^{AB})

\displaystyle=q_{AB}\delta(C^{AC}C_{C}^{B}),

(E.4)

\displaystyle M_{AB}\delta C^{AB}

\displaystyle=M^{AB}\delta C_{AB},

(E.5)

where
M_{AB}
is also an arbitrary symmetric traceless tensor. Considering two variations
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
one can prove the following identity, again with
\delta\sqrt{q}=0
:

T_{AB}\delta\delta^{\prime}q^{AB}=-T^{AB}\delta\delta^{\prime}q_{AB}-T\delta q_{AB}\delta^{\prime}q^{AB},

(E.6)

where
T\equiv q^{AB}T_{AB}
.
It follows from (
E.1
) that

\delta T_{AB}\delta^{\prime}q^{AB}=-\delta T^{AB}\delta^{\prime}q_{AB}+T\delta q_{AB}\delta^{\prime}q^{AB}.

(E.7)

For a traceless tensor such as
M_{AB}
, we have

\delta M_{AB}\delta^{\prime}q^{AB}=-\delta M^{AB}\delta^{\prime}q_{AB}.

(E.8)

For the metric
q_{AB}
itself,
T=2
and

\delta q_{AB}\delta^{\prime}q^{AB}=\delta q^{AB}\delta^{\prime}q_{AB}.

(E.9)

Finally from (
E.5
) we get

\delta M_{AB}\delta^{\prime}C^{AB}=\delta M^{AB}\delta^{\prime}C_{AB}.

(E.10)

While manipulating rank 2 symmetric traceless tensors
C_{AC}
,
M_{AB}
on the sphere, the following identities turn out to be very useful:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(C_{AB}M^{BC}+M_{AB}C^{BC})=\frac{1}{2}{\delta_{A}}^{B}(C_{CD}M^{CD}),

(E.11)

\displaystyle D_{A}C_{BC}M^{BC}=D_{B}C_{AC}M^{BC}+M_{AB}D_{C}C^{BC}.

(E.12)

F   Technical material from quadratic curvature gravity

In this appendix, we review some material about the quadratic curvature gravity theory
[
282
,
281
]
(also coined as new massive gravity theory
[
350
]
). Indeed, the patterns of this theory appear naturally when considering the Weyl charges in the
d=4
case.

The quadratic gravity Lagrangian in
d
dimensions contains generally three pieces

\bm{L}_{QCG}[g]=\bm{L}_{EH}[g]+\beta_{1}\,\bm{L}_{QCG(1)}[g]+\beta_{2}\,\bm{L}_{QCG(2)}[g]

(F.1)

where
g
denotes an arbitrary metric tensor in
d
dimensions,
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
are real constants and

\begin{split}\bm{L}_{EH}[g]&=\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}\left(R+\eta\frac{d(d-1)}{\ell^{2}}\right)\penalty\ \text{d}^{d}x,\\
\bm{L}_{QCG(1)}[g]&=\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}R_{ab}R^{ab}\penalty\ \text{d}^{d}x,\qquad\bm{L}_{QCG(2)}[g]=\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}R^{2}\penalty\ \text{d}^{d}x.\end{split}

(F.2)

Taking the variation of these Lagrangians, we have

\begin{split}\delta\bm{L}_{EH}[g]&=\frac{\delta\bm{L}_{EH}[g]}{\delta g^{ab}}\delta g^{ab}+\text{d}\bm{\Theta}_{EH}[g;\delta g],\\
\delta\bm{L}_{QCG(1)}[g]&=\frac{\delta\bm{L}_{QCG(1)}[g]}{\delta g^{ab}}\delta g^{ab}+\text{d}\bm{\Theta}_{QCG(1)}[g;\delta g],\\
\delta\bm{L}_{QCG(2)}[g]&=\frac{\delta\bm{L}_{QCG(2)}[g]}{\delta g^{ab}}\delta g^{ab}+\text{d}\bm{\Theta}_{QCG(2)}[g;\delta g]\end{split}

(F.3)

where

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\bm{L}_{EH}[g]}{\delta g^{ab}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}\left[R_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}-\eta\frac{d(d-1)}{2\ell^{2}}g_{ab}\right]\penalty\ \text{d}^{d}x,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\bm{L}_{QCG(1)}[g]}{\delta g^{ab}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}\left[2R_{acbd}R^{cd}-D_{a}D_{b}R+D^{c}D_{c}R_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}\left(D^{c}D_{c}R-R_{cd}R^{cd}\right)\right]\penalty\ \text{d}^{d}x,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\bm{L}_{QCG(2)}[g]}{\delta g^{ab}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}\left[2RR_{ab}-2D_{a}D_{b}R+g_{ab}\left(2D^{c}D_{c}R-\frac{1}{2}R^{2}\right)\right]\penalty\ \text{d}^{d}x

(F.4)

and
\bm{\Theta}_{EH}[g;\delta g]=\Theta_{EH}^{a}[g;\delta g]\penalty\ (\text{d}^{d-1}x)_{a}
(idem for
\bm{\Theta}_{QCG(1)}[g;\delta g],\bm{\Theta}_{QCG(2)}[g;\delta g]
) with

\displaystyle\Theta^{a}_{EH}[g;\delta g]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}\left[D_{b}(\delta g)^{ab}-D^{a}(\delta g)^{c}_{c}\right],

\displaystyle\Theta^{a}_{QCG(1)}[g;\delta g]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}\left[2R^{bc}\delta\Gamma^{a}_{bc}-2R^{ab}\delta\Gamma^{c}_{bc}+D^{a}R\delta\ln\sqrt{|g|}+2D_{c}{R^{a}}_{b}\delta g^{bc}-D^{a}R_{bc}\delta g^{bc}\right],

\displaystyle\Theta^{a}_{QCG(2)}[g;\delta g]

\displaystyle=2R\bm{\Theta}_{EH}[g;\delta g]+\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{16\pi G}\left[4D^{a}R\delta\ln\sqrt{|g|}+2D_{b}R\delta g^{ab}\right].

(F.5)

These formulae for the following identifications:
d=4
,
g_{ab}=g^{(0)}_{ab}
,
\beta_{1}=-\frac{\ell^{3}}{4}
,
\beta_{2}=-\frac{1}{3}\beta_{1}=\frac{\ell^{3}}{12}
appear in the sections
3.1.4
and
3.3.1
.
\blacksquare

— Adrien Fiorucci —

Leaky covariant phase spaces

Theory and application to
\bm{\Lambda}
-BMS symmetry

Ph.D. thesis. June 2021.

Supervisors:
Prof. G. Compère & Prof. G. Barnich.

Université Libre de Bruxelles and International Solvay Institutes
CP 231, B-1050 Brussels, Belgium

The present thesis aims at providing a unified description of radiative phase spaces in General Relativity for any value of the cosmological constant using covariant phase space methods. We start by considering generic asymptotically locally (A)dS spacetimes with leaky boundary conditions in the Starobinsky/Fefferman-Graham gauge. The boundary structure is allowed to fluctuate and plays the role of source yielding some flux of gravitational radiation at the boundary. The holographic renormalization procedure is employed to obtain finite surface charges for the whole class of boundary diffeomorphisms and Weyl rescalings. We then propose a boundary gauge fixing isolating the radiative boundary degrees of freedom without constraining the Cauchy problem in asymptotically dS spacetimes. The residual gauge transformations form the infinite-dimensional
\Lambda
-BMS algebroid, which reduces to the Generalized BMS algebra of smooth supertranslations and super-Lorentz transformations in the flat limit. The analysis is repeated in the Bondi gauge in which we identify the analogues of the Bondi news, mass and angular momentum aspects in the presence of a cosmological constant. We give a prescription to perform the flat limit of the phase space and demonstrate how to use this connection to renormalize the corresponding phase space of asymptotically locally flat spacetimes at null infinity including smooth super-Lorentz transformations. In that context, we discuss the memory effects associated with super-Lorentz vacuum transitions and finally provide a new definition of the BMS charges whose fluxes are compatible with soft theorems.

Work supported by the F.R.S.-FNRS (Belgium).
